\begin{document}
\thispagestyle{plain}
{\sffamily\small\bfseries\color{accent}URIEL RESEARCH\hfill 23 NOVEMBER 2026}\par\vspace{14pt}
{\Huge\bfseries\color{ink}A zero and the prime windows\par}\vspace{6pt}
{\Large\color{muted}What one-sided prime sums would have to reveal, and how a sparse supply of arithmetic windows rules it out\par}\vspace{13pt}
\textbf{Abstract.} Suppose a constituent of a positive family of Dirichlet characters has a zero with real part \(\beta>1/2\) in the open critical strip. For \(0<\theta<\beta\), integrate the positive part of the family's main-term-corrected, smoothed prime sum on \([Y,Y^{1+\delta}]\) against \(y^{-1-\theta}\,dy\). A consequence of the pinned localized Landau criterion is that on \emph{every} sufficiently late such interval the mass exceeds \(Y^b\), for any fixed \(b<(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)\). The threshold may depend on \(b\). We derive the extra factor \(1+\delta\) by applying the criterion again on a narrower terminal subinterval. Real and oscillatory pole models recover the exponent.

The arithmetic proof supplies arbitrarily late intervals with something stronger: the \emph{same} family's sum is at most \(y^\theta\) throughout each interval, for a suitably chosen \(\theta>1/2\). Its positive mass is then at most \(\delta\log Y\). We trace which choices stay fixed and which local witnesses can change with the evaluation point. On the actual primitive real character modulo four our continuous finite certificate places the square-root-normalized peak at \(y=487\) on \([256,4096]\). The positive mass uses \(22.05963\%\) of that peak times the logarithmic window length. Continuous spike trains with identical normalized peaks separate the pointwise and integral requirements even on arbitrarily late windows. The real-character measurements are finite tests of the observables. The quantifiers in the contradiction come from the mathematics.

\medskip\textbf{Deliverables.} The single editable source contains this paper, its vector figures, the experimental programs, verification receipts, and instructions for reproducing the results. Original Lean sources are pinned and cited by declaration and line.
\clearpage\tableofcontents
\bigskip\subsection*{The contradiction in two lines}
There is a way to remember the logical content without any code. Write the two inequalities \(G_F(y)\le y^\theta\) and \(W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)>Y^b\) on separate lines. The first inequality has to hold for every \(y\) in a chosen interval. Dividing by \(y^\theta\) and integrating \(dy/y\) turns its positive part into a bound of \(\delta\log Y\). The second inequality has to hold for every sufficiently late start. These statements become incompatible as soon as they refer to the same family on a sufficiently late selected interval. Every substantial piece of the research can be located on one side of that last sentence: establish the unavoidable lower mass, supply the common upper window, or verify that both sides concern the same object.

\clearpage
\section{The collision the experiments test}
Imagine being handed the starting height of an interval on which an arithmetic proof promises a good estimate. A hypothetical zero could have been detected long before that interval, or could manifest itself only far beyond any range we can compute today. To contradict the arithmetic statement we need something that eventually happens \emph{wherever} the next good interval starts. An occasional large prime-sum value would leave the arithmetic free to choose intervals elsewhere. A lower bound on the worst late interval, even a weak one, changes the logic.

Here is a numerical choice of exponents that can be carried through the paper without taking any endpoint limit. Imagine a zero with real part \(3/4\). Measure the positive part of the smoothed prime sum at exponent \(5/8\) on \([Y,Y^{3/2}]\). The zero would force its integrated mass to exceed \(Y^{1/8}\) on every sufficiently late interval. The arithmetic produces arbitrarily late intervals on which that very mass is at most \(\tfrac12\log Y\). Since \(Y^{1/8}/\log Y\) tends to infinity, eventually there is no height at which both claims can hold. The exponent \(1/8\) is a convenient strict choice below the forced threshold \(3/16\). It was deliberately chosen with room to spare. Neither the logical contradiction nor the experiments require deciding what happens exactly at \(3/16\).

\subsection{What is being measured}
The source uses a ramp-weighted sum over prime powers plus an explicit correction. It is a continuous function of a real ordinate \(y\). The smoothing and the correction are what make its transform reflect the logarithmic derivative of the desired \(L\)-function. The function is signed. A one-sided upper bound permits large negative excursions, and positive excursions in different places can be separated by long stretches below zero. Write \(H_\theta(y)=G_F(y)/y^\theta\). The measured positive mass has the especially simple form
\[
 M_\theta(Y)=\int_{\log Y}^{(1+\delta)\log Y}
                  [H_\theta(e^u)]_+\,du .
\]
One can think of this as area above the axis on a plot whose horizontal coordinate is logarithmic. The height of the largest positive excursion and the amount of time spent above zero both matter. Sampling the ends of a plot can miss the peak; knowing the peak alone still leaves its area undetermined.

\subsection{Kinds of evidence}
There are three different experiments here. In a model transform we can insert poles with known locations, change their signs or phases, and calculate the measured mass. In a genuine prime sum we know the arithmetic input and can bound the measured mass between two explicit numbers on an entire finite interval. In the formal source we can follow the quantifiers and replay specific conditional implications. These have complementary strengths. The models can test necessity and sharpness over their stated class. The prime calculation gives actual values for the correct observable. The formal trace tells us which of these features are required on arbitrarily late intervals, a scale a computation cannot exhaust.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}p{.2\linewidth}YY@{}}
\toprule
Test & Controlled change & Failure it would expose\\\midrule
Pole model & Move the pole off the real axis, vary phase, or change its real residue sign. & Treating a signed integral or a real derivative at one frequency as a universal one-sided detector.\\
Spike train & Fix all normalized peak heights and vary their widths. & Inferring a power of integrated mass from a pointwise peak alone.\\
Prime window & Evaluate every prime-power gap and change the normalization sign as a control. & Inferring the interior peak from endpoints or measuring the wrong function.\\
Arithmetic witness & Change a local index jointly or use two incompatible indices at one point. & Substituting pointwise existence of pieces for a compatible estimate throughout the window.\\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}
\end{center}
The source of all statements concerning the existing formalization is the original commit beginning \texttt{40e56bab}. Section headings and code names in this paper refer to that revision. The models and new consequences are identified separately. A theorem checked for the prime-family interface uses the original analytic result as an import. This study does not claim a new proof or a full rebuild of the Riemann hypothesis.



\section{What a zero would force the primes to do}\label{sec:zero-analytic}
Take the Riemann zeta function first. Imagine a nontrivial zero \(\rho=\beta+i\gamma\) with \(1/2<\beta<1\). We want a statement about what someone measuring primes on the positive real axis would have to see. The answer involves a signed, smoothed prime count, its \emph{positive part}, and an entire interval. It says something about every sufficiently late interval of a fixed power width, even though the arithmetic construction supplies its upper bounds on only a selected collection of intervals. That asymmetry is what makes the contradiction possible.

\providecommand{\wzlink}[3]{\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/#1.lean\#L#2}{#3}}
\subsection{The observable before the transform}
Write \(\Lambda(n)=\log p\) when \(n=p^j\) for a prime \(p\) and \(j\ge1\), and zero otherwise. The conductor-one observable, for \(y\ge1\), is
\begin{equation}
 G(y)=\sum_{n\le y}\Lambda(n)(y/n-1)
                -y\log y+(1+\gamma_{\rm E})y. \label{eq:wz-g}
\end{equation}
A prime power first enters at \(y=n\) with weight zero; its influence increases linearly thereafter. Thus \(G\) is continuous even at the entry points. The logarithmic subtraction and linear correction remove the principal main terms. A reader inspecting raw counts of primes, or omitting the prime powers, would be looking at a different observable. For a nonprincipal character \(\chi\) the linear weights also contain \(\chi(n)\); the correction becomes \(yL'(1,\chi)/L(1,\chi)\). In the common theorem the measured real function is \(G_F(y)=\sum_i a_i\operatorname{Re}G_{\chi_i}(y)\), where \(F\) is a finite family, every \(a_i>0\), and a principal character is admitted only at modulus one. This specializes to \eqref{eq:wz-g} with one term and weight one. \wzlink{CharFamily}{10}{Original observable, family and one-sided hypothesis, lines 10--45.}

Fix \(0<\theta<\beta\) and \(\delta>0\). On the interval \(I_\delta(Y)=[Y,Y^{1+\delta}]\), \(Y>1\), define the weighted positive mass
\begin{equation}
 W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)=\int_{I_\delta(Y)}[G_F(y)]_+\, y^{-\theta-1}\,dy
 =\int_{I_\delta(Y)}\left[\frac{G_F(y)}{y^\theta}\right]_+\frac{dy}{y},
 \qquad [v]_+=\max(v,0).                                  \label{eq:wz-mass}
\end{equation}
This integral measures both height and duration on a logarithmic horizontal axis. Negative values pay no cost. A value at one ordinate has zero mass by itself. A wide region of moderate positive excess can pay more than a narrow region of large excess. The interval has logarithmic length \(\delta\log Y\), which continues to grow.

The literal \texttt{WindowHyp} quantifies over any nonnegative integrable weighted majorant \(R\) that dominates \(G_F\) on the interval. Its least pointwise choice is \([G_F]_+\). This choice is admissible on every compact interval inside \(y\ge1\): there are only finitely many summands to consider there, each ramp is continuous and starts at zero, and the corrections and weight are continuous. Integrating the least choice proves that the majorant formulation is equivalent, for arbitrarily late starts, to
\begin{equation}
 \forall\eta>0\ \ \forall Y_0>1\ \ \exists Y\ge Y_0,\qquad
 W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)\le Y^\eta.                             \label{eq:wz-window}
\end{equation}
The bound can be tested on a different subsequence of starts for each \(\eta\). No uniform choice of a finite start for all \(\eta\) appears here. The local compactness argument also matters logically: the existence of a failed majorant condition alone would not distinguish excessive integral from nonintegrability. \wzlink{CharFamily}{41}{Source quantifiers and weighted integrability.}

\paragraph{What fails in the presence of the zero.}
For any of the family's characters with a zero at \(\rho\), the localized criterion implies the negation of \eqref{eq:wz-window}. Written with its quantifiers outward, this is the following assertion:
\begin{equation}
 \exists\eta>0\ \ \exists T>1\ \ \forall Y\ge T,\qquad
                         W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)>Y^\eta.           \label{eq:wz-force}
\end{equation}
The power and threshold may depend on the family, \(\theta\) and \(\delta\). The result forces positive mass on \emph{each} sufficiently late window, so the arithmetic cannot escape the obstruction by selecting especially quiet windows farther out. It allows negative values and zeros of the observable within such a window. It does not give the sign at any previously nominated ordinate or the location of the first forced excursion. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/lemmaL}{13}{Original implication from windows to nonvanishing, lines 13--22.}

\subsection{A quantitative form using the upper end of the window}
The real part \(\beta\) has so far entered only through \(\theta<\beta\). The criterion applies for \emph{each} positive power width and \emph{each} test exponent below \(\beta\). Exploiting both freedoms yields a power-scale lower bound for the same observable.
\begin{equation}
 \liminf_{Y\to\infty}
       \frac{\log W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)}{\log Y}
                \ \ge\ (1+\delta)(\beta-\theta).                \label{eq:wz-liminf}
\end{equation}
The logarithm of the mass in this formula is used only for sufficiently late starts; positivity there follows from \eqref{eq:wz-force}. This deduction compares several instances of the original localized criterion.

To prove it, choose \(\theta<s<\beta\) and \(1<a<1+\delta\), both fixed. The terminal part of the original window is itself a window of strictly positive power width. Set \(Z=Y^a\) and \(\delta'=(1+\delta)/a-1>0\); then
\[
 I_{\delta'}(Z)=[Y^a,Y^{1+\delta}]\subset I_\delta(Y).
\]
Apply \eqref{eq:wz-force} to \(s,\delta'\). There are \(\eta>0,T>1\) such that \(W_{s,\delta'}(Z)>Z^\eta\) for all \(Z\ge T\). On this subinterval the original weight differs by a factor \(y^{s-\theta}\ge Z^{s-\theta}\). Nonnegativity of the integrand gives
\begin{equation}
 W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)
 \ \ge\ Z^{s-\theta}W_{s,\delta'}(Z)
 \ >\ Y^{a(s-\theta+\eta)} \qquad (Y^a\ge T).                  \label{eq:wz-bootstrap}
\end{equation}
For each \(b<(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)\) we can first choose \(a,s\) with \(a(s-\theta)>b\), and then take \(Y\) sufficiently large for that choice. This is exactly \eqref{eq:wz-liminf}. No estimate uniform as \(s\uparrow\beta\) or \(\delta'\downarrow0\) was used. Sending parameters to their limits inside the finite threshold would be an extra assertion. For the source window \(\delta=1/2\) the exponent is at least \(3(\beta-\theta)/2\).

It is instructive that the factor \(1+\delta\) comes from the upper end of the \emph{available interval}. A single application at the original start, even with the improved weight \(s\), would only recover \(\beta-\theta\) by this argument. The universal quantifier over late starts permits shifting the start. The ability to use a narrower but still fixed-width window permits keeping the upper end fixed. These are properties of the analytic implication. No arithmetic supply of good windows for the auxiliary widths \(\delta'\) is needed in this deduction; the hypothetical zero itself forces their failure.

There are two immediate interpretations. First, \(\sup_{y\in I_\delta(Y)}[G_F(y)/y^\theta]_+\) is at least \(W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)/(\delta\log Y)\). Thus positive excursions beat any fixed normalized pointwise allowance on every sufficiently late window. Second, a universal improvement of the exponent in \eqref{eq:wz-liminf} would require an additional hypothesis. The abstract transform of \(g(u)=e^{\beta u}\) has a single positive-residue pole at \(\beta\) and satisfies the crude growth condition below. Its mass is exactly
\[
 \frac{Y^{(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)}-Y^{\beta-\theta}}{\beta-\theta}.
\]
This gives equality in \eqref{eq:wz-liminf} for the analytic model. Its input is an exponential rather than a sequence of primes. Sharpness within that abstract class conveys no arithmetic example of an off-line zero. The bound also leaves the coefficient at the endpoint power undetermined.

\paragraph{Formal status of the strengthening.}
The supplemental module \texttt{WindowMass.lean} proves local integrability of the actual weighted positive part, \eqref{eq:wz-force}, the ordered suffix-integral comparison, and the quantified power statement in \eqref{eq:wz-liminf}. Its last declaration gives an eventual strict inequality for every exponent strictly below the displayed rate. The logarithmic limit inferior is its mathematical reformulation. The formal integrability proof uses measurability and the imported growth bound on a compact interval rather than the finite-ramp continuity argument above. The new module calls the pinned \texttt{lemmaL} directly. Each of its four declarations has the reported axiom inventory \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice}, and \texttt{Quot.sound}. Twenty-eight original project sources and compiled artifacts in the dependency closure were authenticated. The supplementary kernel replay uses these imports and the pinned Mathlib cache. It does not rebuild the arithmetic construction that supplies a favorable window. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/lemmaL}{13}{Analytic input to the supplementary theorem.}

\subsection{What can be inferred about individual excursions?}
Two frequently conflated claims concern the height of a maximum and the share of the interval on which large values occur. The first follows from the forced mass without a sign-pattern assumption. Let \(h(y)=[G_F(y)]_+/y^\theta\) and \(L=\delta\log Y\). For each \(b<(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)\) we eventually have \(\max_{I_\delta(Y)}h>Y^b/L\). Somewhere in every sufficiently late window the normalized signal beats every fixed constant. The maximizing ordinate can depend on the window and on the family, and the result yields no sign information at either endpoint. Indeed, for any \(\theta<s<\beta\), applying \eqref{eq:wz-force} at \(s\) also gives a point \(y\) in each sufficiently late window with \(G_F(y)>y^s\): otherwise \(W_{s,\delta}(Y)\le L\). The same reasoning works on every fixed terminal subwindow used in \eqref{eq:wz-bootstrap}.

For the second claim, write \(\mu(E)=\int_E dy/y\), \(H=\max_{I_\delta(Y)}h\), and \(E_\lambda=\{y\in I_\delta(Y):h(y)>\lambda\}\) for \(\lambda\ge0\). Splitting the positive mass gives
\[
 W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)\le\lambda L+H\mu(E_\lambda).
\]
An upper bound for the peak turns a lower bound for mass into a lower bound for logarithmic width. The crude source growth gives at most \(H\le C_F Y^{(1+\delta)(2-\theta)}\). With \(b<(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)\), these estimates only force a width of at least \((Y^b-\lambda L)/(C_F Y^{(1+\delta)(2-\theta)})\) when the numerator is positive. That lower bound can tend to zero. Hence these particular estimates do not provide a positive fraction of a window on which the signal is large. At the arithmetic interface, bounding height at \emph{every} ordinate requires no separate width estimate, whereas an attempted replacement with exceptional ordinates must control their contribution in height times logarithmic width. A count of unrelated exceptional arithmetic indices supplies neither factor by itself.

One can also start from an explicit finite measurement. Knowing \(W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)\) to many decimal places at one \(Y\) tests the measurement and its control function but leaves the quantifier \(\forall Y_0\,\exists Y\ge Y_0\) open. Conversely, a hypothetical zero never directly prescribes the visible sign of the complete prime sum at the experimenter's favorite \(Y\). A single-pole model can predict such a sign and can reveal interference by putting a second pole beside the first. The analytic theorem is deliberately phrased in terms that survive every other singularity allowed in the actual family.

\section{How a hidden zero becomes visible to a one-sided measurement}
\subsection{The pole at the transform level}
The ramp in \eqref{eq:wz-g} is chosen so its Mellin transform is particularly simple. For \(\operatorname{Re}s>1\), integrating the linear function \(y/n-1\) from \(n\) to infinity gives
\[
 \int_n^\infty (y/n-1)y^{-s-1}\,dy=\frac{n^{-s}}{s(s-1)}.
\]
The weighted prime sum then brings in the logarithmic derivative of the relevant \(L\)-function. In the conductor-one case the transform includes the explicit regularization
\[
 \Phi(s)=-\frac{1}{s(s-1)}
       \left(\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}
       +\frac{1}{s-1}+1-(1+\gamma_{\rm E})s\right).
\]
For a nonprincipal character replace the bracket by
\(L'(s,\chi)/L(s,\chi)-sL'(1,\chi)/L(1,\chi)\). The displayed integral formulae are initially obtained in their convergence half-plane. Local meromorphic descriptions then allow us to examine what happens at a candidate zero in the critical strip. The principal correction matters around \(s=1\); it does not cancel a pole at an interior zero. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/MellinRamp}{10}{Ramp integral.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/mellinGchiModOne}{115}{Principal normalization.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/mellinGchiOfNeOne}{116}{Nonprincipal normalization.}

Near a zero of multiplicity \(m>0\), factor \(L(s)=(s-\rho)^m h(s)\) with \(h(\rho)\ne0\). Then \(L'/L=m/(s-\rho)+h'/h\). The transform has a \emph{simple} pole whose residue is \(-m/[\rho(\rho-1)]\), regardless of the multiplicity. Taking the real part of the observable averages the transforms for a character and its inverse. With family weights, the residue becomes
\[
  c_\rho=-\frac{S_\rho}{\rho(\rho-1)},\qquad
  S_\rho=\sum_i\frac{a_i}{2}\bigl(m_i(\rho)+m_i^{\rm inv}(\rho)\bigr)>0 .
\]
All terms in the real numerator are nonnegative; at least one is positive. Coincident poles of several family members enhance the same coefficient. At distinct poles, different phases can still interfere in the original data or at a poorly chosen transform center. The strict positivity is in \(S_\rho\). For a nonreal pole its complex residue can have a phase. A real pole in \(0<\rho<1\) has positive real residue because \(\rho(\rho-1)<0\). The sign of a real pole will be essential in using a one-sided window estimate. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/LogDerivLocal}{9}{Local multiplicity.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/poleData}{336}{Family residue and its real-axis sign, lines 336--400.}

\subsection{A high derivative that looks inside the window}
Change variables \(u=\log y\), put \(U=\log Y\), and write \(g(u)=G_F(e^u)\). The interval is \([U,(1+\delta)U]\); its weighted positive mass is \(\int [g(u)]_+ e^{-\theta u}\,du\) there. The Mellin transform becomes a Laplace transform on \(u\ge0\). The source obtains \(|g(u)|\le C_0e^{2u}\), so for \(\sigma>2\) the normalized derivatives have convergent integral representations
\[
 A_k(\sigma+it)=\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}\Phi^{(k)}(\sigma+it)
 =\int_0^\infty g(u)e^{-itu}\frac{u^ke^{-\sigma u}}{k!}\,du .
\]
The derivative supplies the factor \(u^k\) and normalization removes the factorial scale. After normalization to unit mass, the real kernel has mode \(k/\sigma\), mean \((k+1)/\sigma\) and standard deviation \(\sqrt{k+1}/\sigma\). By increasing \(k\) in proportion to \(U\) the mode moves into the window. The standard deviation grows only like \(\sqrt U\), whereas the distance to a window edge can grow like \(U\). \wzlink{LandauTheorems/poleData}{248}{Growth and Laplace representation.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/IteratedDerivLaplace}{84}{Differentiation under the integral.}

This heuristic localization has to withstand \(e^{2u}\) growth outside the interval. Small probability under the normalized kernel alone would be insufficient if the quantity multiplied by it were large on the tail. The source's exponential tail estimate weights that possible growth before integrating. One choice visible in the proof is \(\ell=2+6/\delta\), \(\sigma=\ell(1+\delta)(2+\ell)\) and \(k=\lceil(1+\delta/2)\sigma U\rceil\) for \(U\ge1\). Then \(k\ge U\) and the contribution of the growth-weighted kernel away from the good interval, before multiplication by \(C_0\), is at most \(C/\sigma^k\). These constants suffice for existence. For example \(\delta=1/2\) gives \(\ell=14,\sigma=336\) and \(k=\lceil420U\rceil\). A small finite simulation using a different \(\sigma\) illustrates the same mechanism only after that difference is acknowledged. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/landauTails}{80}{Explicit tail choices and derivative order, lines 80--104.}

\paragraph{Why a lower bound on \(g\) is unnecessary.}
Let \(w_k(u)=u^ke^{-\sigma u}/k!\). The triangle inequality gives \(|A_k(\sigma+it)|\le\int|g|w_k\). Adding \(\operatorname{Re}A_k(\sigma)=\int g w_k\) changes the integrand to \(|g|+g=2[g]_+\). On a favorable window the positive part is bounded by its majorant. Off the window the tail estimate applies. On the window itself,
\[
  \sup_{u\ge0}\frac{u^ke^{-(\sigma-\theta)u}}{k!}
           \le(\sigma-\theta)^{-k}
\]
follows, for instance, from the \(k\)-th term of the exponential series. The result is the same derivative inequality simultaneously at every real frequency \(t\):
\begin{equation}
 |A_k(\sigma+it)|+\operatorname{Re}A_k(\sigma)
          \le C'\,\frac{e^{\eta U}}{(\sigma-\theta)^k}.          \label{eq:wz-derivative}
\end{equation}
Here \(\sigma,C'\) are chosen before \(\eta\) and the start threshold. The favorable \(U,k\) are then selected before \(t\). Interchanging the last two choices would let the phase alter the window and defeat the subsequent comparison. The negative part may be large at individual ordinates; the real-center term accounts for it. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/landauWindowIneq}{27}{One-sided integral inequality.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/landauWindow}{40}{Uniform-frequency derivative bound and quantifiers.}

\subsection{Why moving the center overcomes oscillation}
A simple pole \(c/(s-\rho)\) contributes exactly \(c/(z-\rho)^{k+1}\) to \(A_k(z)\). If it is the unique pole in a disk about \(z\), removing its principal part leaves an analytic function there. For a radius \(e\) strictly between the pole distance \(d=|z-\rho|\) and the disk boundary, Cauchy's estimate gives an error at most \(M/e^k\). Thus the normalized high derivatives can isolate a pole through the gap between distances, even where the original sum displays interference. At a center equidistant from a conjugate pair the leading terms can interfere through phase for arbitrarily large derivative orders. The proof therefore has to construct its center as well as its large derivative order. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/LandauPoleAsym}{19}{Principal-part approximation with a radius gap.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/LandauNearest}{36}{Excluding tied nearest poles at a shifted center.}

In one branch the real center has a sufficiently large pole-free disk, and a vertical shift admits a unique closer pole. This pole may be different from the one originally hypothesized. The shifted coefficient is large in norm; a Cauchy estimate at the real center bounds how much its real-part correction in \eqref{eq:wz-derivative} can subtract. In the other branch the real center has a unique nearest real pole. Its positive residue forces a \emph{positive} real leading term there, while the norm term is nonnegative. This explains the sign condition: \(g(u)=-e^{\beta u}\) would have an inadmissible negative real residue and zero positive mass on every window. A genuinely nonzero residue without its real-axis sign condition cannot imply the proposed positive excursions. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/LandauDichotomy}{24}{The two ways of finding a detectable pole.} \wzlink{LandauTheorems/landauWindow}{83}{Complex and real branch estimates, lines 83--166.}

In both branches the two estimates for a coefficient force
\[
       c_0d^{-k}\le M/(e_{\rm rad})^k
             + C'e^{\eta U}(\sigma-\theta)^{-k},
      \qquad 0<d<e_{\rm rad}\le \sigma-\theta,\quad c_0>0 ,
\]
Choosing \(\eta=\tfrac12\log(e_{\rm rad}/d)>0\) and using \(k\ge U\) makes the right side divided by \(d^{-k}\) tend to zero as \(U\) increases. Arbitrarily late windows contradict that inequality. The distance comparison explains what a hypothetical zero would do to the measured prime sum: its singularity would keep depositing enough positive weighted mass to make every sufficiently late window fail. In a model containing just one conjugate pair, the observed oscillation can be assigned entirely to those poles. The prime-sum theorem concerns the aggregate positive mass, allowing all other singularities and their phases to be present. \wzlink{LandauTheorems/LandauGrowthContra}{9}{Choice of the loss exponent and the large-order contradiction.}



\section{How arithmetic covers an entire interval}
\label{sec:arith_windows}

The analytic contradiction concerns a single real-valued observable at all real arguments in arbitrarily late intervals. The arithmetic proof builds fields, rows, retained sets and chain data. Several of these objects can change with the ordinate. To see how these statements meet, consider three choices together. The character we are testing fixes the observable. A family of arithmetic data adapted to the requested exponent supplies intervals. A local witness at which two estimates hold simultaneously bounds the observable at a specified point. Treating all three as one fixed witness would impose a stronger demand than the source makes. Allowing unrestricted changes in all three would destroy the inference.

\subsection{One character and one observable}
Fix a primitive Dirichlet character \(\psi\). The cyclic level \(\Lambda=\mathrm{cyc}(\psi)\) uses the subgroup generated by \(\psi\). Its retained primitive-character list contains \(\psi\) itself. From this one level the adapter builds a finite analytic family \(F\). The principal member of conductor one receives weight one. Each other member receives its strictly positive level weight. Admissibility follows because a primitive principal character has conductor one. The source proves two identifications at the same level,
\[
    G_F(y)=\Lambda.G_m(y)\quad\text{for every real }y,\qquad
    \mathrm{ZeroFree}(F,\theta)
          \ \longleftrightarrow\ \Lambda.\mathrm{ZeroFreeAbove}(\theta).
\]
Here \(G_F\) includes the normalization of the principal summand and the real parts of the nonprincipal summands. One need not bound the absolute value of every character contribution on the same interval. A one-sided bound on this fixed weighted sum is handed to the analytic theorem. The noncancellation argument in the analytic half is what permits a zero of a particular member to be detected through the common sum. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/ExplicitFormulaErrorOfHostConditions.lean#L121-L125}{\textsf{[Cyclic level]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/LevelFamily.lean#L27-L52}{\textsf{[Level adapter]}}

An arithmetic family \(M_\varepsilon\) is a different object. It can depend on a small loss exponent \(\varepsilon\). Its data include a set \(S\) of real scales, a family threshold, a type of possible host indices \(I(y)\) for each scale \(y\), the arithmetic data at each index, and explicit agreements among components. The basic structure already requires that \(S\) be unbounded and \(I(y)\) be inhabited on \(S\). A separate property strengthens unboundedness to
\begin{equation}
 \forall B\in\mathbb R\ \exists Y\ge B\quad [Y,Y^\lambda]\subseteq S .
 \label{eq:arith-full}
\end{equation}
This strengthening matters. Powers of two form an unbounded set, but contain no nondegenerate real interval. In the actual character-specific family class the interval assertion is accompanied by an assertion about \emph{all} indices at every sufficiently large scale. The character data include both an explicit-formula compatibility predicate at the fixed cyclic level and a cohomological defect bound for the field of the host. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/FamCore.lean#L71-L95}{\textsf{[Family data]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/FamCore.lean#L155-L156}{\textsf{[Scale window]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/Supply.lean#L22-L23}{\textsf{[Supply hypotheses]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/Supply.lean#L42-L44}{\textsf{[Character predicates]}}

\subsection{Where the three-halves window originates}
At an earlier stage the cohomological input \(\mathrm{VWinAt}(\psi,401)\) controls a moving tower page. Fix \(0<\varepsilon<1/52\) and choose a page function \(e(x)\) obeying
\[
       x^{2+8\varepsilon}\le 2^{e(x)}\le 2x^{2+8\varepsilon},
       \qquad x\ge1.
\]
The input supplies \(J\ge1\) and a threshold in an outer scale \(X\). For \(X\) beyond that threshold and each eligible prime pair at scale \(X^{1/J}\), it gives \(W\ge X^{1/J}\) with \(W^2\le X\). The cohomological estimate holds \emph{throughout} \([W,W^2]\), with exponent 401 in its integer cutoff and with the page function evaluated at the local ordinate. A prime-pair theorem also supplies bounds on a range of ordinates for auxiliary characters with at least one nontrivial quadratic twist. This auxiliary twisted-character input has a different target from the final weighted sum attached to \(\psi\). \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ExistsPageN.lean#L27-L49}{\textsf{[Finite exceptions]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/PairGood.lean#L18-L30}{\textsf{[Paired twists]}}

There is a specific reason the two spatial scales fit together. A field construction contributes a scale factor \(c\ge1\); choose \(k\ge1\) by \(k^{2+8\varepsilon}=c\). The underlying prime pair \(q,d\), the window start \(W\), and \(k\) have been chosen before the target ordinate \(x\) is introduced. The proof takes \(Z=kW\) as the new start and arranges \(W\ge k^3\) and \(W\ge k^{400}\) in advance. It then covers
\[
              kW\le x\le(kW)^{3/2}
\]
both at \(x\) and at the rescaled ordinate \(x'=x/k\). Indeed,
\[
     (kW)^{3/2}\le W^2
          \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k^3\le W
          \qquad(W>0,\ k\ge1).
\]
The left inequality puts \(x\) below \(W^2\). The lower endpoint puts \(x'\) above \(W\), and \(x'\le x\). Thus the entire target interval and every requested rescaled ordinate are within the range required for this part of the construction. Since \(W\) can be taken above any prescribed bound, the resulting \(Z\)'s occur arbitrarily late. The theorem ultimately returns the existence of suitable data at every \(x\); reading its body shows that the same \(q,d\) works across this particular interval, while \(e(x/k)\) can vary. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean#L23-L52}{\textsf{[Scale choice]}}

The second lower bound on \(W\) pays a different cost. In transferring a cutoff from page \(x'\) to \(x\), the target cohomological sum runs over positive integer indices strictly below \(\lfloor x^{400}\rfloor\) and the input controls positive indices strictly below \(\lfloor (x')^{401}\rfloor\). Before taking floors the exact condition is
\begin{equation}
   (kx')^{400}\le(x')^{401}
       \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k^{400}\le x',\qquad x'>0.
   \label{eq:arith-cutoff}
\end{equation}
The extra unit of exponent on the incoming side absorbs the finite rescaling, once all ordinates lie above \(k^{400}\). It need not absorb that rescaling at a small finite scale. For example \(k=2,W=8\) gives \((kW)^{3/2}=W^2\) exactly but fails the pre-floor cutoff inequality at \(x'=W\) by a factor \(2^{397}\). Taking \(W=2^{400}\) meets both tests. This integer is a diagnostic of the margins in the proof, not an asserted start of a number-theoretic window. The outer threshold can also depend on \(\psi,\varepsilon,J\) and the other size conditions. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/WinPairs.lean#L52-L81}{\textsf{[Endpoint fit]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/TwistForm.lean#L19-L26}{\textsf{[Twist data]}}

The host-supply theorem constructs its set of good scales from the existence of the data returned at each \(x\). It then uses the field realization, rank admissibility and host estimates to produce an actual arithmetic host for the given scale, with explicit-formula compatibility and a cohomological bound on that host's field. The auxiliary prime pair being shared in the construction just described does not mean one field and one selected chain are held constant over the entire proof. At a later interface only the good-scale set and its covering property are required. Finally the original top assembly instantiates \(\lambda=3/2\), along with the hypotheses named \texttt{GLq.Green} and \texttt{VWin}, and an inhabited package of global compatibility conditions named \texttt{HypsAlt}. Our trace inspects these uses; it does not independently construct the initial prime pairs or the cohomological intervals. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ShortNorms/supplyOfAdmOf.lean#L49-L58}{\textsf{[Host construction]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ShortNorms/supplyOfAdmOf.lean#L110-L135}{\textsf{[Family assembly]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ThetaCorrespondence/ThetaPairLifts/ThirdPlaceSeries/TopPlug.lean#L12-L18}{\textsf{[Supply composition]}}

\subsection{One local host index does the work at each ordinate}
The family-selection step is a conjunction of requirements. Divisibility, irreducibility, rank comparison, a cohomological bound, and a global chain estimate must apply to compatible data. The final assembly uses loss indices \(e_D=2,e_L=0\). It chooses a common admissible small-\(\varepsilon\) range, obtains a set of admissible parameters, and obtains a family for those parameters. The row and global clauses are applied to that same family. The character-specific class supplies its windows and its all-index explicit-formula estimate, with constant 4 in the latter. A bound valid for an independent family picked for each clause would leave an existence problem at the point where the conjunction is consumed. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/ThetaCorrespondence/ThetaPairLifts/ThirdPlaceSeries/TopShape.lean#L47-L55}{\textsf{[Shape parameters]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/DesWin.lean#L11-L31}{\textsf{[Supplied window]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/CharacterBoxes/ZeroFreeAllOfTopG.lean#L16-L33}{\textsf{[Final specialization]}}

Now fix this family. Above a threshold \(x_E\), explicit compatibility \(E(y,i)\) holds for every \(i\in I(y)\). Above a chain threshold \(x_C\), for \(y\in S\) the tied-chain assertion gives an index \(i(y)\) for which the core bound \(H(y,i(y))\) holds. Part of the proof checks that the retained set entering this core is exactly the retained set of that host. The scale and index in the explicit estimate can therefore be specialized to \(y,i(y)\). At the transition to the scalar inequality, the same data witness both premises. Schematic notation makes the order visible:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \forall y\ge x_E,\ \forall i\in I(y)\quad &E(y,i),\\
 \forall y\ge x_C,\ y\in S\ \Longrightarrow\
             &\exists i\in I(y)\quad H(y,i),\\
 \forall y\ge x_H,\ \forall i\in I(y)\quad
       E(y,i)\land H(y,i)&\ \Longrightarrow\ 
       \Lambda.G_m(y)\le y^{1/2+(e_D+e_L+7)\varepsilon}.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The threshold \(x_H\) in the last line is chosen before the particular host and retained set. In the original proof its value combines separate thresholds from the main comparison, the large-scale assumptions and the final remainder absorption. Both the fixed family and this uniformity are essential. A fresh \(x_H\) chosen after each \(y\) could make the implication empty. The proof of the scale-window estimate takes the maximum of these thresholds. Every \(y\) in a window beyond that maximum is covered. The index \(i(y)\) remains free to vary because the resulting left side depends only on \(\Lambda,y\). \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/LevelGmLeOnFamilyScales.lean#L13-L30}{\textsf{[Tied estimates]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/WindowInequalityOfArtinInputs.lean#L11-L25}{\textsf{[Common host]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/CoreGm.lean#L12-L15}{\textsf{[Core and explicit data]}}

The last unit in the coefficient of \(\varepsilon\) has a concrete job. In the body of \texttt{core\_gm} the main comparison controls an error with exponent \(1/2+(e_D+e_L+6)\varepsilon\), alongside a term with coefficient \(4\Lambda.o\) and smaller exponent \(1/2+\varepsilon\). Above a level-dependent threshold, \(1+4\Lambda.o\le y^\varepsilon\). After bringing both terms under the larger power, that inequality absorbs their combined coefficient, changing the exponent loss from \(e_D+e_L+6\) to \(e_D+e_L+7\). For the supplied \((e_D,e_L)=(2,0)\) this yields \(9\varepsilon\). This is a sufficient bound in the assembly. Proving that any part of the loss is necessary would require examples saturating the preceding estimates. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/CoreGm.lean#L48-L74}{\textsf{[Remainder absorption]}}

One can see the logic fail in both directions with just two ordinates and two possible host indices per ordinate. At each ordinate record two subsets \(E,H\subseteq\{0,1\}\) of passing indices. Each subset has four choices, giving \(4^4=256\) tables at two ordinates. We deliberately allow \(E\) to be any subset in this counterfactual so that weakening the original all-index property can be tested. In the exhaustive run 81 tables have both predicates separately realizable at both ordinates; 49 have a common local index at each ordinate; 31 have one index valid at both ordinates. Thus 32 tables lose the needed local conjunction if separate existences are allowed. Requiring a constant index across ordinates would exclude 18 extra tables despite pointwise compatibility. These are counts in a synthetic space of truth assignments, not frequencies of selected arithmetic hosts.

The counts can be verified by hand. For one ordinate, \(3^2=9\) pairs are separately nonempty. Of the 16 pairs, exactly \(3^2=9\) have empty intersection: each index can belong to neither set, to \(E\) only, or to \(H\) only. Thus 7 pairs have nonempty intersection. Squaring yields 81 and 49. A specific index present in both predicates at both ordinates fixes four of the eight bits, leaving \(2^4=16\) tables. Inclusion--exclusion for the two indices gives \(16+16-1=31\). This test isolates the distinction between selecting a different witness to bound the \emph{same observable} and changing the observable. For the latter error take two candidate target vectors \((2,0)\) and \((0,2)\). Their pointwise minimum passes ceiling 1 at both sites, although neither fixed target passes. Changing hosts is legitimate only because the comparison returns the same \(\Lambda.G_m(y)\) in every case.

\subsection{From the exponent margin to the mass margin}
Suppose the candidate zero has real part \(\beta>1/2\) in the open critical strip. The original conversion to a zero-free statement tests
\[
 \theta=\frac12+\frac12\min\!\left(\beta-\frac12,\frac12\right).
\]
Then \(1/2<\theta<\beta\) and \(\theta<1\). On the concrete route one chooses \(\varepsilon>0\) below the family cutoff such that \(9\varepsilon\le\theta-1/2\). The general small-loss lemma handles any real coefficient \(C\) using the minimum of half the allowed cutoff and \((\theta-1/2)/(|C|+1)\). Only after this choice does the proof select \(M_\varepsilon\). Above the joint threshold and above one, monotonicity of \(y^\alpha\) in \(\alpha\) gives \(G_F(y)\le y^\theta\) for every \(y\) in each selected interval. The cutoff in \(y\) can depend on \(\varepsilon\); the argument asks only for cofinal windows after \(\varepsilon\) is fixed. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Formalism/SharpRestriction/RamificationFrobenius/TotientGaloisClasses/TopWin.lean#L59-L81}{\textsf{[Exponent choice]}}

The adapter invokes its analytic result with multiplier 1 and width \(\delta=\lambda-1=1/2\). The nonnegative envelope \(R(y)=y^\theta\) has normalized weighted mass exactly
\[
       \int_Y^{Y^{3/2}} R(y)y^{-\theta-1}\,dy
             =\int_Y^{Y^{3/2}}\frac{dy}{y}
             =\frac12\log Y .
\]
For every \(\eta>0\) there is a threshold beyond which this is at most \(Y^\eta\). The covering property lets the proof request a new window starting beyond the maximum of the user's cutoff and this threshold. Here \(\varepsilon\) controls the arithmetic exponent, \(\eta\) measures the loss permitted in the positive-mass criterion, and \(\delta\) controls the width of the window. Their quantifiers have different roles. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/PrimitiveCosets/Imports.lean#L8-L19}{\textsf{[Window adapter]}} \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/LandauTheorems/WindowHypOfRpow.lean#L31-L57}{\textsf{[Integrated majorant]}}

There is a stronger consequence of the arithmetic input that makes the collision with an off-line zero especially clear. Fix any \(0<g<\theta-1/2\) and choose \(\varepsilon\) still smaller, so \(1/2+9\varepsilon\le\theta-g\). This change can select a different family \(M_\varepsilon\), while the measured character family \(F\) remains fixed. On the resulting cofinal windows,
\begin{equation}
 \int_Y^{Y^{3/2}}\max\{G_F(y),0\}\,y^{-\theta-1}\,dy
       \ \le\ \int_Y^{Y^{3/2}} y^{-1-g}\,dy
       =\frac{Y^{-g}-Y^{-3g/2}}{g}.
 \label{eq:arith-strongmass}
\end{equation}
The right side tends to zero. The source adapter uses the logarithmic estimate above; equation~\eqref{eq:arith-strongmass} is a written deduction that preserves spare exponent margin. It does not assert an estimate at exponent \(1/2\), a uniform window over all \(\theta\), or a uniform threshold as \(g\) approaches \(\theta-1/2\). A hypothetical member zero demands excess positive mass on every sufficiently late window at the tested \(\theta\). Arithmetic can produce a window later than that putative cutoff with positive mass as small as desired. Both claims concern the same weighted function and the same window width, so they cannot coexist.

One last control tests what this observation does and does not buy. Write \(u=\log y\). The positive-mass integrand becomes \(G_F(e^u)_+ e^{-\theta u}\,du\). Normalize a test interval in \(u\) to unit length and let this normalized positive profile be a triangular tent of height 8. Width \(1/16\) gives area \(1/4\); width \(1/2\) gives area 2. Against a normalized mass budget of 1 the same peak has opposite mass verdicts, although both violate the pointwise ceiling of 1. The calculation distinguishes height from integrated cost. The arithmetic trace supplies a pointwise inequality on a continuum of ordinates, so it does not need a separate estimate of widths. Obtaining a new proof using only aggregate arithmetic control would require an estimate of the \emph{weighted measure in the real variable \(y\)} where individual bounds are weak. A count of exceptional primes, arithmetic indices or individual integer weights does not on its own bound that measure.

\paragraph{Scope of the kernel check.}
We compiled and independently replayed four new declarations against Lean 4.33.1 and the original analytic module for the pointwise-window implication. Before compiling, the 30 analytic object digests were checked against the published values recorded in the previous analytic replay. The new declarations prove same-index specialization with a variable local index, its composition with the actual \texttt{zeroFree\_of\_rpow\_window} theorem, and the two scope-preserving scalar equivalences for the squared endpoint fit and the pre-floor cutoff. Their stated axiom inventories are \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound}. The additional arithmetic proof-body trace authenticates 22 original source files and 36 selected spans at the pinned Git tree. The upstream assertions of common family supply and compatible local core data are inputs to this new kernel check. No numerical output in these tests instantiates that entire arithmetic construction.

\paragraph{How much geometric room is left?}
The local fitting condition generalizes cleanly. If the arithmetic construction has an outer interval \([W,W^2]\) and selects \(Z=kW\), then \([Z,Z^\lambda]\) fits at its upper end exactly when \(W^{2-\lambda}\ge k^\lambda\). With \(k>1\) and \(1<\lambda<2\) this requires \(W\ge k^{\lambda/(2-\lambda)}\). The threshold \(W=k^{400}\) already used for the separate cutoff puts equality at \(\lambda=800/401\). Thirty supplementary integer comparisons confirmed the endpoint and both sides, including the need for larger \(W\) for larger \(\lambda\). At \(\lambda=2\) this particular embedding cannot work for \(k>1\). This calculation explains the margin at one upstream geometric step. The assembled source uses \(\lambda=3/2\); extending the local fit does not widen the arithmetic theorem.


\section{An oscillating pole with an adversarial phase}
A single real pole with positive residue gives the simplest test of the lower exponent: it produces a positive exponential in logarithmic time, so there is no cancellation to overcome. A nonreal pole introduces a different difficulty. The signal can spend the start, the end, or nearly half of a long interval below zero. We asked whether the predicted power persists even when the starting phase is chosen to minimize the measured positive area. In this experiment the transform is known and the mass can be integrated over every lobe. There is no inference from an apparent oscillation in prime data to a new zero.

Fix parameters \(\beta>\theta\), \(\omega>0\), \(a=\beta-\theta\) and let
\[
   g_\phi(u)=e^{\beta u}\cos(\omega u+\phi),\qquad
   \Phi_\phi(s)=\tfrac12\left(\frac{e^{i\phi}}{s-\beta-i\omega}+
                           \frac{e^{-i\phi}}{s-\beta+i\omega}\right).
\]
These two poles have real part \(\beta\). The residues are conjugate, and there is no pole on the real axis. For \(U=\log Y,\ V=(1+\delta)U,\ L=V-U\), measure
\[
 M_\phi(U)=\int_U^V e^{au}[\cos(\omega u+\phi)]_+\,du.
\]
Set \(\psi=\omega V+\phi\), the phase at the far edge. Normalize the mass by \(e^{aV}\). The normalized observable depends on the phase, \(L\), and \(a,\omega\), without an exponential factor obscuring the geometry of the lobes.

\subsection{A lower bound that holds for every phase}
Write \(P=2\pi/\omega\). Once \(L\ge P\), the window contains a full final period \([V-P,V]\). Throughout that period \(e^{au}\ge e^{a(V-P)}\), and the integral of the positive part of the cosine over a full period is \(2/\omega\), independently of the phase. Consequently
\[
       \frac{2e^{-aP}}{\omega}\le e^{-aV}M_\phi(U)
           \le \frac{1-e^{-aL}}{a}.
\]
The lower constant can be crude; its positivity and independence of the phase do the work. The limit of \(\log M_\phi(U)/U\) is \((1+\delta)a\), uniformly even over phase choices varying with \(U\). This is an exact result for this particular conjugate pair. A prime-family transform can contain many poles and a substantial analytic remainder. The formal proof uses distance from a shifted center to isolate a pole in that broader setting.

\subsection{Where the worst phase actually occurs}
We can compute the limiting coefficient rather than bound it by a full period. Define the past-weighted periodic function
\[
 J(\psi)=\int_0^\infty e^{-av}[\cos(\psi-\omega v)]_+\,dv .
\]
Splitting at \(v=L\) gives the exact finite-window identity
\begin{equation}
     e^{-aV}M_\phi(U)=J(\psi)-e^{-aL}J(\psi-\omega L).
     \label{eq:phase}
\end{equation}
In particular the error from retaining only \(J(\psi)\) is uniformly bounded by \(e^{-aL}\max J\). The terminal phase matters because the weight tilts the observed past; an unweighted average over complete periods would eliminate this effect.

Set \(E=e^{-a\pi/\omega}\) and \(T=\psi+\pi/2\bmod 2\pi\) in \([0,2\pi)\). During the positive half-cycle \(0\le T\le\pi\) we have
\[
 J(\psi)=\frac{a\cos\psi+\omega\sin\psi+
          \omega e^{-aT/\omega}/(1-E)}{a^2+\omega^2}.
\]
During the negative half-cycle it decays as
\[
 J(\psi)=\frac{\omega e^{-a(T-\pi)/\omega}}{(a^2+\omega^2)(1-E)}.
\]
These formulas solve \(\omega J'=[\cos\psi]_+-aJ\) with periodic boundary conditions. They also locate the global extrema without relying on a list of sampled phases. At \(-\pi/2\) the derivative is negative; at zero it is positive because \(J(0)<1/a\); at \(\pi/2\) it is negative again. At any critical point in the positive lobe, \(\omega J''=-\sin\psi\). Each critical point in \((-\pi/2,0)\) is a strict minimum, and each in \((0,\pi/2)\) a strict maximum. This gives exactly one of each, since two minima would require a point between them where the derivative changes sign in the other direction. The negative half-cycle is strictly decreasing.

With \(\beta=3/4,\theta=1/2,\delta=1/2,\omega=2\), the minimum occurs near \(-1.3161913\) radians with \(J_{\min}\simeq1.0074527\); the maximum near \(1.1719407\) has \(J_{\max}\simeq1.5534562\). We tested 256 evenly spaced phases together with both stationary phases at each of seven window starts. Directly clipping and integrating individual lobes and evaluating equation~\eqref{eq:phase} differed by at most \(6.7\times10^{-16}\) in normalized mass. The decimal agreement is a floating-point check. The uniform mathematical guarantee comes from the formulas above.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}\toprule
\(U=\log Y\)&Least sampled & Largest sampled & Uniform lower estimate & \(\log M_{\min,\mathrm{sample}}/U\)\\\midrule
4&.068945&.835557&.065234&$-.293610$\\
8&.552105&1.147629&.435968&.300748\\
16&.800917&1.402797&.797215&.361125\\

The three middle columns use terminal normalization \(e^{-aV}\). The fourth evaluates \(J_{\min}-e^{-aL}J_{\max}\), rounded here for display. The last uses the unnormalized mass at the least sampled phase. At the earliest window even its logarithmic sign can be misleading. Late values approach the predicted exponent \(3/8\); those slightly above \(3/8\) also contain a positive coefficient term divided by \(U\). Nothing in the general theorem requires monotonic approach to its bound.

\caption{Phase sensitivity after dividing out \(e^{(\beta-\theta)V}\). The dashed lines are the extremal coefficients of the infinite-past expression. The observations and the uniform finite-width correction use the same normalization.}\end{figure}

\subsection{Three ways of making an actual pole look quiet}
First, both endpoints can be negative while a full positive lobe lies inside. At log width \(P\) take \(\psi=\pi\); both cosines are \(-1\). Our clipped integral nevertheless gives normalized positive mass \(0.67770086\) in the stated pair model. Second, one can make the \emph{signed} weighted integral exactly zero while the positive mass survives. In complex notation it is \(e^{aV}\operatorname{Re}(e^{i\psi}D)\) with
\[
                 D=\frac{1-e^{-(a+i\omega)L}}{a+i\omega}.
\]
Choosing \(\psi=\pi/2-\arg D\) makes the real part vanish. At \(U=64\) the measured normalized positive mass for that phase is \(1.26470347\). The tiny signed remainders in the machine record are numerical roundoff. Third, if the log width \(L\) stays fixed below half a period, one can choose an infinite sequence of starts for a fixed signal that keeps the entire window in a negative lobe. The positive area on those windows is exactly zero. The formal criterion fixes \(\delta>0\), giving \(L=\delta U\) that grows without bound. Replacing that width by a fixed factor \(y\in[Y,cY]\) permits a qualitatively different experiment.

\section{The derivative experiment in the transform plane}
The same two-pole signal allows an independent view of the detection mechanism. Normalize the \(k\)th derivative as \(A_k(z)=(-1)^k\Phi^{(k)}(z)/k!\). With phase zero its exact value is
\[
 A_k(z)=\tfrac12(z-\beta-i\omega)^{-k-1}
             +\tfrac12(z-\beta+i\omega)^{-k-1}.
\]
At a large real center the two conjugate contributions can oppose one another. At \(z=\sigma+i\omega\) the pole at the same height lies at distance \(\sigma-\beta\). Its partner is farther away at \(\sqrt{(\sigma-\beta)^2+4\omega^2}\). The closer pole eventually dominates the high derivative. For the present parameters, the nearest distance from the real center, \(\sqrt{(\sigma-\beta)^2+\omega^2}\), also exceeds \(\sigma-\theta\) when \(\sigma=4\). This makes the real-center contribution decay after normalization. In this example the combined one-sided expression has an especially transparent exponential rate.

Set \(E=\sigma-\theta=3.5\) and form
\[
       B_k=E^k\bigl(|A_k(\sigma+i\omega)|+\operatorname{Re}A_k(\sigma)\bigr).
\]
The experiment evaluates this expression independently in Cartesian and polar coordinates. At order 4 it is \(0.03043\); at 32 it is \(1.66131\); at 128 it is approximately \(2026.40\); at 512 it exceeds \(4.63\times10^{15}\). The real-center contribution at order 512 is only \(1.51\times10^{-20}\) on that scale. The asymptotic rate \(\log B_k/k\) tends to \(\log(3.5/3.25)\simeq0.074108\), with a finite value \(0.070452\) at \(k=512\). The leading factor \(1/(2(\sigma-\beta))\) accounts for most of the finite-order difference. These calculations concern a fixed frequency and two specified poles; the formal dichotomy selects its comparison centers from the actual pole configuration.

The sign test supplies a useful falsification. For \(g(u)=-e^{\beta u}\) the transform has a real pole to the right of \(\theta\) with a negative residue. The signal has zero positive mass on every interval. In this case \(A_k(\sigma)\) is negative real and the combination at frequency zero is exactly zero. More generally \(|A_k(\sigma+it)|\le |A_k(\sigma)|\) and the norm-plus-real-part expression is nonpositive. This model excludes a version of the criterion that would omit positivity of real-pole residues. It is an admissible model of that weakened condition; the prime-family residues have the sign required by the original theorem.

\subsection{How a derivative puts its weight inside the window}
The normalized density \(r^{k+1}u^k e^{-ru}/k!\) is a gamma density of shape \(k+1\). Its mode is \(k/r\). The proof must handle both the ordinary kernel and the kernel tilted by the available \(e^{2u}\) global envelope. In the experiment we used \(\sigma=64\), \(k=\lceil1.25\sigma U\rceil\), and \(r=\sigma\) or \(r=\sigma-2\). The values were chosen to place both modes inside \([U,1.5U]\). They are separate from the \(\sigma=4\) test of the two-pole derivative formula.

For integer shape \(m=k+1\), the left tail at \(u\) is the upper Poisson tail \(\Pr\{\mathrm{Pois}(ru)\ge m\}\); the right tail at \(v\) is \(\Pr\{\mathrm{Pois}(rv)\le m-1\}\). We sum each small tail directly from its largest endpoint term using the log of that term and a scaled recurrence. Computing the left tail by subtracting a number near one would return numerical zero prematurely. The program checks the bounds obtained by optimizing the gamma moment-generating function, namely \(\exp[-m(x-1-\log x)]\) at \(x=ru/m\) or \(rv/m\) on the appropriate side of 1.

At \(U=128\) the base-10 logs of left and right tail probabilities are respectively \(-104.73\) and \(-80.25\) for \(r=64\), and \(-134.80\) and \(-54.59\) for \(r=62\). The growth tilt shifts weight toward the right edge. A small tail fraction alone does not establish the source's absolute derivative bound: the normalization \(r^{-k-1}\), the bound on \(g\), and the constants needed for the pole contradiction still have to be put on the same scale. The source performs those steps in its tail and window lemmas. The table tests the underlying localization for a finite choice of parameters.



\section{Can the peak fail everywhere while the mass passes?}
The prime data distinguish peak and area on a finite interval. There is a stronger question about the two hypotheses as statements of existence. A single narrow bump can make one nominated interval bad for a pointwise bound. It does not prevent the pointwise theorem from choosing a different interval. To separate the two hypotheses at the level of their \emph{quantifiers}, one function must defeat the pointwise test on \emph{every} sufficiently late interval while still meeting the mass test arbitrarily far out. We built such functions, with exactly the same normalized peaks in all cases.

Let \(u=\log y\), \(c=1/8,\ h=1/4,\ w_n=c e^{-bn}\) and \(T(t)=(1-|t|)_+\). For \(b>0\) set
\[
 H(u)=\sum_{n\ge1}e^{hn}T((u-n)/w_n),\qquad g(u)=e^{\theta u}H(u).
\]
The tents are disjoint, \(H\) is continuous, and each integer \(n\) gives normalized peak \(e^{hn}\). Each sufficiently late \([U,(1+\delta)U]\) contains many integer centers. Whatever constant \(C\) is chosen for \(g(u)\le C e^{\theta u}\), every sufficiently late window fails this pointwise comparison. This conclusion holds for all three choices of \(b\) in the experiment.

\subsection{A width threshold visible on the real axis}
The normalized area of one complete spike is \(w_ne^{hn}=c e^{(h-b)n}\), since the unit tent has total integral one. Write \(k=h-b\). Sum over complete spikes to bound the window mass below and over all spikes meeting the interval to bound it above. Only the two edge spikes can be clipped. The interior contains consecutive integers from \(U+O(1)\) to \(V+O(1)\) with \(V=(1+\delta)U\). Uniformly in the start, as \(U\) tends to infinity this gives
\[
   \int_U^V H(u)\,du=
       \begin{cases}
         \Theta(e^{kV}) & k>0,\\
         c\delta U+O(1) & k=0,\\
         \Theta(e^{kU}) & k<0 .
       \end{cases}
\]
For \(k>0\) the last few complete spikes supply both the scale and the relevant end of the interval. For \(k<0\) the first few dominate. At \(k=0\) every complete spike contributes the same area; at integer window ends the two half-spikes together supply one full area. The latter two families meet the general mass criterion on all sufficiently late windows for every positive \(\eta\), despite violating the fixed pointwise envelope on all of them.

Here are three functions observed on the \emph{same} logarithmic window from 32 to 48 with \(\theta=1/2\). Their common normalized maximum is \(e^{12}=162754.7914\). Their integrated masses differ by more than six orders of magnitude.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\toprule
Width exponent \(b\)&Rightmost pole real part& Mass on \([32,48]\)&Mass on \([128,192]\)\\\midrule
\(1/8\)&\(5/8\)&349.284732&\(2.65147\times10^{10}\)\\
\(1/4\)&\(1/2\)&2.000000&8.000000\\
\(1/2\)&\(1/4\)&0.000165516&\(6.36503\times10^{-15}\)\\\bottomrule\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\cyclefigure{.96\linewidth}{widths}\caption{All three model functions have the same peak on each plotted interval. The remaining curves plot the total positive area on the log window. Logarithms of the peak and area are shown separately.}\end{figure}
The code integrates each clipped tent in its own rescaled coordinate. At a large center \(n\), adding an extremely small \(w_n\) to \(n\) in floating point can produce \(n\) again. Forming \((U-n)/w_n\) and \((V-n)/w_n\) before clipping avoids treating that spike as a point of zero area. Runs with starts shifted by \(3/8\) test that the trend survives changing which edge spikes are cut.

\subsection{The width threshold is also a pole threshold}
The model affords a further check on the meaning of positive mass. The location of its rightmost pole can be obtained from the Laplace transform independently of the real-axis area argument. On the initial half-plane of absolute convergence,
\begin{align*}
 \Phi(s)&=c\sum_{n\ge1}e^{-(s-\theta-h+b)n}\,
       K((s-\theta)w_n),\\
 K(z)&=\int_{-1}^1(1-|t|)e^{-zt}\,dt
      =\frac{2(\cosh z-1)}{z^2},\qquad K(0)=1 .
\end{align*}
Put \(\beta_c=\theta+h-b\). Splitting off \(K(0)\) gives
\[
     \Phi(s)=\frac{c}{e^{s-\beta_c}-1}+
       c\sum_{n\ge1}e^{-(s-\beta_c)n}\bigl(K((s-\theta)w_n)-1\bigr).
\]
The remainder is analytic for \(\operatorname{Re}s>\beta_c-2b\). Indeed \(K(z)-1=O(z^2)\) uniformly near zero, and uniform convergence on compact subsets follows from the resulting extra factor \(e^{-2bn}\). The first term has simple poles at \(\beta_c+2\pi i m,\ m\in\mathbb Z\), each with residue \(c\). The remainder cannot cancel them and the original sum is analytic farther to the right. Thus the horizontal location is a property of the model transform rather than a value inferred by fitting a line through a handful of window masses.

For \(b<h\) the real pole exceeds \(\theta\) and the growth exponent of the mass is exactly \((1+\delta)(\beta_c-\theta)\). Varying only the widths moves the pole across the line without changing the peak heights. The numerical transform check compared the explicit geometric separation with a direct long sum at four positive distances \(\epsilon\) from \(\beta_c\). At \(\epsilon=1/256\) the three values of \(\epsilon\Phi(\beta_c+\epsilon)\) lie between \(0.12475\) and \(0.12476\), close to the exact residue \(1/8\). Agreement tests the implementation. The analytic continuation and nonzero residue follow from the displayed series.

This example sharpens the question we should ask of the arithmetic. Counts of exceptional positions in a finite group concern a different variable from the ordinate \(y\). To replace a pointwise arithmetic estimate by an integral one, one would need to show how much \emph{logarithmic length in \(y\)} the excursions occupy and how high the normalized sum can be there. The existing pointwise proof already suffices. The continuous train demonstrates exactly what a genuinely weaker replacement could permit. It supplies no arithmetic theorem transferring orbit counts into excursion widths.

\section{How long can a pole hide from finite data?}
The original source implies a lower mass bound after a threshold depending on the observable and the test exponent. It does not return an observational cutoff. A delayed-pole control makes this limitation visible in the same units as the finite prime experiment.

Set \(T=\log8192,\ \theta=1/2,\ \beta=3/4\). In log coordinates let
\[
  g_T(u)=
     \begin{cases}
      0,&u<T,\\
      e^{\beta(u-T)}-e^{-(u-T)},&u\ge T .
     \end{cases}
\]
The onset is continuous and the tail is nonnegative. Its Laplace transform equals
\[
        e^{-sT}\left(\frac1{s-\beta}-\frac1{s+1}\right).
\]
The real pole at \(\beta\) has positive residue \(e^{-\beta T}\), and its influence can be delayed beyond any preassigned finite list of windows by increasing \(T\). One can even match a specified continuous trace through the end of a finite observation range: extend that trace continuously to zero before \(T\), and add \(g_T\). The compact piece has entire Laplace transform and cannot erase the pole. This argument describes the flexibility of an abstract observable; it does not give another \(L\)-function with freely assignable prime data.

The positive mass of this tail is elementary. Put \(l=\max(U,T)\). When \(l<V\) its mass is
\[
        e^{-\beta T}\int_l^V e^{(\beta-\theta)u}\,du
             -e^{T}\int_l^V e^{-(1+\theta)u}\,du .
\]
It vanishes when \(V\le l\). We checked nine starts with \(U/T\) from \(1/4\) to 6, using an independent Simpson integration for nonempty support. At the tested start \(U/T=2\) the mass still fits the moderate bound \(e^{0.1U}\); at \(U/T=3\) it exceeds it, \(113.38\) against \(14.93\). At \(U/T=6\) the mass is about \(296238\), against \(222.86\). The reported crossover is the first tested exceedance on this finite grid. The existence of an eventual exceedance follows analytically from the exponential with rate \((1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)>0.1\). No inference about the threshold of an actual prime-family transform follows by matching these illustrative scales.

A finite pointwise failure is likewise compatible with a theorem promising sufficiently late selected windows. This is the two-sided lesson of the control. A passing plot cannot certify all future windows, and one bad plot cannot defeat a cofinal existence theorem. The two quantifiers must be made to meet before a contradiction follows.



\section{Testing the two window statistics on actual prime powers}
\label{sec:actual-prime-mass}

The model experiments put a singularity where we choose it. The complementary experiment holds the arithmetic fixed and measures precisely what the two window tests see. Use a single character with weight one, first the character of modulus one and then the real primitive character modulo four. Both are admitted by the pinned family definition. A prime contributes when its power enters the ramp, with its arithmetic von Mangoldt weight. We do not insert a model zero into either calculation. The purpose is to distinguish a pointwise maximum, an integral of positive mass, and a pair of sampled endpoints at a scale where all three are computable.

Write \(G\) for the real-valued function of the selected character. On \(I=[Y,Y^{3/2}]\) take
\[
 H_\theta(y)=\frac{G(y)}{y^\theta},\quad
 P=\max_{y\in I}H_\theta(y),\quad
 E=\max\{H_\theta(Y),H_\theta(Y^{3/2})\}.
\]
A window admissible in the analytic criterion has an integrable nonnegative majorant. On a finite interval our continuous function has a canonical least one, \(G_+(y)=\max(0,G(y))\). Its weighted mass and comparison with the best constant peak envelope are
\[
 M=\int_I G_+(y)y^{-\theta-1}\,dy
   =\int_{\log Y}^{(3/2)\log Y}\max(0,H_\theta(e^u))\,du,
 \qquad
 R=\frac{M}{P_+\,(\log Y)/2}.
\]
Here \(P_+=\max(0,P)\), and we report \(R\) only when \(P>0\). The length factor is forced by the variable of integration. The pointwise transfer gives \(M\le P_+(\log Y)/2\). Equality for a continuous positive peak would require attaining that peak throughout the log window. Thus \(R\) measures average normalized height relative to maximum normalized height, with negative ordinates clipped to zero. It is not the fraction of the window on which the sum is positive. The control \(G(y)=y^\theta\) has \(P=1\) and \(M=(\log Y)/2\), which at \(Y=256\) equals \(\log16>2.77\). Dropping the log-width factor would fail even for this simplest saturating example.
\href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005/src/Development/AutomorphicArithmetic/DedekindLFunctions/CharFamily.lean\#L10-L45}{Pinned ramp, family and window definitions.}

\subsection{Design and the arithmetic normalization}
Before the new evaluations, the protocol fixed \(Y=16,64,256\), \(\delta=1/2\), \(\theta=1/2,3/4\), the two singleton families, the statistics and the controls. The first exponent supplies the familiar square-root normalization; the second is strictly above the critical line, as in the argument that excludes zeros to its right. A previously certified conductor-one peak on the final window was available as a positive control. The tests were that the full continuous peak bounds the mass with its log-width factor, that at least one peak exceeds both endpoint observations, and that at least one mass uses less than 95 percent of its peak envelope. These are finite questions at the specified scales. A later refinement for conductor four used its piecewise affine form after seeing the initial enclosures; it is reported separately.

For the conductor-one case,
\[
 G_1(y)=\sum_{p^j\le y}\log p\,(y/p^j-1)
                   -y\log y+(1+\gamma)y .
\]
For \(\chi_4(n)=0\) on even integers, \(1\) on residues \(1\bmod4\), and \(-1\) on residues \(3\bmod4\),
\[
 G_4(y)=\sum_{p^j\le y}\chi_4(p^j)\log p\,(y/p^j-1)+cy,
 \qquad c=\frac{L'(1,\chi_4)}{L(1,\chi_4)}.
\]
In particular \(\chi_4(p^2)=1\) for every odd prime, even when \(\chi_4(p)=-1\). Attaching the sign of the prime to each of its powers changes the experiment. Numerical evaluation of the log derivative is also consequential: an error \(e\) in \(c\) induces a normalized error \(e y^{1-\theta}\). This grows with the ordinate. An apparent smooth bias in the prime sum might therefore come entirely from its linear correction.

Here is an elementary, independent route to the constant for this character. For \(s>0\), the Dirichlet beta function has the Mellin integral
\[
 \beta(s)=\frac1{\Gamma(s)}
       \int_0^\infty \frac{t^{s-1}e^{-t}}{1+e^{-2t}}\,dt .
\]
Expanding the denominator into consecutive powers of \(-e^{-2t}\) first proves the formula for \(s>1\) with absolute convergence; the integral is analytic near 1 and agrees with the analytic continuation there. Set \(z=(1-e^{-2t})/2\in[0,1/2]\). Then \((1+e^{-2t})^{-1}=\tfrac12\sum_{j\ge0}z^j\), and truncating at \(j=m-1\) changes the factor by at most \(2^{-m}\). After binomial expansion, the two quantities used in the code are
\begin{align*}
  \beta(1)&=\sum_{j<m}2^{-j-1}
       \sum_{r=0}^j(-1)^r{j\choose r}\frac1{2r+1}+r_0,\\
  \beta'(1)&=-\sum_{j<m}2^{-j-1}
       \sum_{r=0}^j(-1)^r{j\choose r}\frac{\log(2r+1)}{2r+1}+r_1 .
\end{align*}
The first remainder satisfies \(0\le r_0\le2^{-m}\). In the derivative, the integrand gains the factor \(\log t+\gamma\) from \(t^{s-1}/\Gamma(s)\) at 1. The integral of \(e^{-t}|\log t|\) is at most \(1+2/e<2\), by bounding its two parts at \(t=1\); also \(0<\gamma<1\). Consequently \(|r_1|\le3\,2^{-m}\). Differentiation under the integral is justified in a neighborhood of 1 by an integrable power times \(1+|\log t|\) near zero and exponential decay at infinity. This calculation uses neither a table of zeros nor a guess of the correction from the observed prime data.

At \(m=55\) the resulting outward enclosures are
\[
\begin{array}{rl}
\beta(1) &\in[0.78539816339744830,\;0.78539816339744834],\\
\beta'(1)&\in[0.19290131679691234,\;0.19290131679691251],\\
c &\in[0.2456095847773140,\;0.2456095847773143].
\end{array}
\]
For a second normalization check we enclosed both ordinary alternating sums between adjacent partial sums after 8,192 terms. For the derivative the summand magnitudes \(\log(2r+1)/(2r+1)\) decrease from \(r=1\); for the original beta value the decrease starts at \(r=0\). Alternating differentiation at \(s=1\) follows by uniform alternating convergence of the differentiated series on a compact real neighborhood of 1, after discarding finitely many initial terms. The coarse ratio interval \([0.245223035,0.245996207]\) contains the entire accelerated one. As a negative control, changing \(+cy\) to \(-cy\) forces the whole normalized \(\chi_4\) peak on \([256,4096]\), at \(\theta=1/2\), below \(-7.72955\). This artificial sign reversal annihilates all positive mass on that window. It would completely change what the one-sided test sees.

\subsection{Certifying all real ordinates on each window}
Two independent enumerations, a sieve of prime powers and direct trial division through 4096, agree before any weighted sum is computed. Splitting at each prime power yields continuous segments. On the nonprincipal segments \(G_4(y)=ay-b\); on the principal segments \(G_1(y)=ay-b-y\log y\). A new prime power contributes zero right at its own knot. The values at common endpoints agree despite the slope changes. For the principal constant we use the harmonic-log limit with the elementary enclosure
\[
  \frac1{2(n+1)}\le H_n-\log n-\gamma
                  \le\frac1{2n}+\frac1{2n^2},\qquad n=10^6.
\]
For example, the difference of consecutive values of \(H_k-\log k\) is \(\int_k^{k+1}(u-k)u^{-2}\,du\); summing upper and lower square-reciprocal estimates gives these bounds. Logarithms are evaluated by power-of-two range reduction to \(1\le x\le2\), followed by 55 terms of the positive series for \(2\operatorname{atanh}((x-1)/(x+1))\) and its geometric remainder. The fixed-point denominator is \(2^{160}\). Integer square roots from each side give rigorous enclosures for \(y^{1/2}\) and \(y^{3/4}\). Signed interval operations and reported decimal endpoints round outward. \href{https://github.com/nousentllc/riemann-hypothesis/blob/b616d08a805e05e358ad0d2b3cd7b180e058a19e/research/uriel/experiments/second/FINITE_BOUND.md}{Earlier conductor-one certificate and harmonic-constant argument.}

On a subinterval \([x,x+h]\) of a smooth piece, let \(C\) be the chord joining the two exact endpoint values of \(G\). For the principal sum, \(G''(y)=-1/y\), so
\[
   C(y)\le G(y)\le C(y)+\frac{h^2}{8x}.
\]
Indeed the concave function lies above its chord and its deviation is bounded by \((y-x)(x+h-y)/(2x)\). For the nonprincipal sum the chord is exact and the additive tolerance is zero. On each subinterval the computation uses lower enclosures for both endpoints of the lower chord and upper enclosures for both endpoints of the upper chord. If a chord with endpoint values \(u,v\) straddles zero, its positive-part area is precisely \(h\max(u,v)^2/(2|u-v|)\). The same-sign cases give a trapezoid or zero. Multiplying the lower area by \((x+h)^{-1-\theta}\) and the upper area by \(x^{-1-\theta}\) bounds the weighted integral on that entire subinterval. The maximum of the upper chord and the range of \(y^\theta\) give a separate upper bound on the peak. Values actually evaluated at cell endpoints provide lower witnesses. The subinterval width is no more than its left endpoint divided by 2,048. Aggregate sign bounds permit unresolved crossing cells; those cells contribute valid enclosing areas too.
