\noindent The table rounds the certified intervals outwards. The windows overlap and the two exponent choices reuse the same underlying sums. The rows therefore serve as deterministic controls for distinct inequalities; interpreting them as twelve independent statistical trials would misrepresent the design. Every mass interval has relative width below 0.4 percent. All twelve ratios are strictly below 0.95. In eight rows the interior peak is certified to exceed both endpoint values. The other four comparisons are unresolved or admit an endpoint maximizer by these enclosures. The principal square-root peak on the last window contains the earlier narrower certificate \([0.1177870105,0.1177870108]\).

\begin{figure}[htbp]\centering\cyclefigure{.97\linewidth}{actual}
\caption{The signed normalized character sum on the largest finite window. Positive shading corresponds to positive mass in logarithmic time. The figure samples the curve; separate interval computations locate the exact winning ordinate and bound its area.}\end{figure}
\subsection{A closed integral and an exact maximizer at conductor four}
An additional argument after the primary measurements yields substantially sharper certificates for the second character. For a single affine piece the derivative of \((ay-b)y^{-\theta}\) has the sign of \((1-\theta)ay+\theta b\). A strictly interior local maximum would require this linear expression to cross downward through zero. That requires \(a<0,b>0\), and then \(ay-b<0\) throughout the positive half-line. A constant zero derivative gives no strict interior maximum; if it vanishes identically, \(a=b=0\) since \(0<\theta<1\). Thus a positive global maximum on an affine spline is attained at an interval endpoint or a knot. This addresses the normalized function itself; merely checking knots without this argument would miss possible interior maxima for other character normalizations.

To compute the positive mass independently, bound the true affine function below by \(a_{\rm lo}y-b_{\rm hi}\) and above by \(a_{\rm hi}y-b_{\rm lo}\), with rational coefficients coming from the outward constant and prime-log enclosures. For either bounding line, split exactly at \(y=b/a\) if it crosses zero. The primitive on its positive piece is
\[
     \int(ay-b)y^{-1-\theta}\,dy
       = \frac{a}{1-\theta}y^{1-\theta}
           +\frac{b}{\theta}y^{-\theta} .
\]
Zero slopes and zero-valued endpoints are handled directly by the signs. Each of the six closed-integral and knot-peak enclosures is contained in its corresponding primary enclosure. This second algorithm shares the elementary logarithm, root and normalization certificates. It changes both the prime-power enumeration used to generate knots and the treatment of every interval, eliminating numerical quadrature from the conductor-four cross-check.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrr@{}}
\toprule $Y$ & $\theta$ & Unique maximizing $y$ & $P$ (rounded) & $M$ (rounded) \\
\midrule
16 & $1/2$ & 23 & .2329733234 & .1956626420\\
16 & $3/4$ & 19 & .1067848315 & .0847871114\\
64 & $1/2$ & 67 & .1686422570 & .1325403398\\
64 & $3/4$ & 67 & .0589450995 & .0382334776\\
256 & $1/2$ & 487 & .1299174295 & .0794604553\\
256 & $3/4$ & 487 & .0276557333 & .0144425007\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
On \([256,4096]\), at the square-root scale, the maximum of the two observed endpoint values is below \(0.050190\); the full maximum exceeds \(0.129917\). The larger of the endpoint observations captures less than 39 percent of the true peak. The integrated positive mass uses only \(22.05963\) percent of the bound one would get by extending that true peak constantly across the log window. With \(\theta=3/4\), the corresponding share is \(18.83527\) percent. These are large differences in both directions on the identical arithmetic data: endpoint-only observation understates the strongest local demand, while extending a genuine peak uniformly can overstate the actual integral demand. Even in the conductor-one rows where the entire function was certified positive, the rare attainment of its peak can leave substantial distance between the two tests.

The same sign roots permit one more distinction. Let \(f\) denote the fraction of the logarithmic window on which \(G_4>0\). On each strictly positive interval \([u,v]\) its occupied length is \(\log(v/u)\), independent of \(\theta\). On the largest window \(f\in[0.613573398155,0.613573398156]\). Conditional on positivity, the mean value of \(H_{1/2}\) is between \(0.359527119856\) and \(0.359527119857\) times the peak, with log-uniform weighting. Their product gives the observed \(R\). Positive excursions therefore occupy over three fifths of the window in logarithmic time, while their typical height in this mean is materially below their peak. The small total-to-peak ratio alone does not diagnose narrow spikes. At \(\theta=3/4\) the support fraction stays the same and the conditional mean-to-peak ratio falls to \(0.30697658051\). This extra check uses exactly the same arithmetic ordinates and isolates what the choice of normalization changes.

The arithmetic implication uses a pointwise estimate on arbitrarily late windows for positive character families built by its construction. This finite experiment uses two specified singleton families and three nominated windows. We neither extract the formal construction's selected windows nor identify their heights or their positive-mass ratios. The finite data verify how to evaluate the quantities that meet at the interface and show the measurable cost of a pointwise substitute. Passing any finite list of these tests cannot exclude a zero to the right of the critical line. Conversely, the hypothetical pole argument concerns what must eventually happen on \emph{every} sufficiently late window for at least one allowed loss exponent. No conclusion about that quantifier follows from the apparent decrease across these three starting heights.


\section{What the comparison buys, and what it leaves open}
\subsection{Reading the implication in the reverse direction}
The exponent in equation~\eqref{eq:wz-liminf} can be used as an instrument for evaluating a proposed arithmetic estimate. Fix the family and \(0<\theta<1,\delta>0\). Suppose that, along an unbounded sequence of starts, the measured positive mass can be proved to have growth at most a fixed power \(\lambda\ge0\) up to arbitrarily small exponent losses. Then a constituent zero in the open strip must obey
\begin{equation}
              \operatorname{Re}\rho\ \le\ \theta+\frac{\lambda}{1+\delta}.
              \label{eq:inverse-budget}
\end{equation}
Here the premise means that for every \(\epsilon>0\) there are arbitrarily late \(Y\) with \(W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)\le Y^{\lambda+\epsilon}\). A single unbounded sequence with this property in the limiting upper-exponent sense suffices; a different sequence for each \(\epsilon\) also suffices. To verify the claim, a zero strictly to the right of the proposed boundary would leave room for a \(b\) strictly between \(\lambda\) and \((1+\delta)(\operatorname{Re}\rho-\theta)\). Choose \(\epsilon<b-\lambda\), move beyond the threshold for that \(b\), and use an arithmetic window there. The proposed upper and forced lower powers contradict one another. When the displayed boundary reaches or exceeds 1, the resulting assertion imposes no further restriction inside the open strip.

The statement describes an asymmetry important for interpreting experiments. A proved subsequence of small masses can exclude zeros beyond a line. Observing a large mass does not identify a zero or its height; the deduction supplies no such converse. No finite set of measured small masses establishes this subsequence. The scale of \(y\), the choice of \(\theta\), the width \(\delta\), and the identity of the measured family all form part of the proposed certificate.

There is a second way to read the factor \(1+\delta\). At the upper edge of the window \(y\) is of order \(Y^{1+\delta}\), so changing the weight from \(y^{-\theta}\) to \(y^{-s}\) can save nearly \(Y^{(1+\delta)(s-\theta)}\) on a terminal subwindow. The original analytic theorem is available at every fixed positive power width. One can move the subwindow arbitrarily close to that far edge in the exponent while preserving positive width. This order of choices produces the denominator in equation~\eqref{eq:inverse-budget}. Taking a subwindow of zero width from the outset would supply no analytic information.

\subsection{The price of replacing pointwise control by a small exceptional set}
Write \(H_\theta=G_F/y^\theta\) and let \(\mu(E)=\int_Edy/y\). Suppose, for one fixed family and arbitrarily late selected windows, a proposed independent argument gives \(H_\theta\le C\) off a measurable set \(E_Y\), \(H_\theta\le Y^h\) on \(E_Y\), and \(\mu(E_Y)\le Y^{-d}\). Assume \(C\ge0\) is fixed and the same window satisfies all three bounds. Then
\[
       W_{\theta,\delta}(Y)\le C\delta\log Y+Y^{h-d}.
\]
If \(h\le d\) this suffices for zero exclusion to the right of \(\theta\). If \(h>d\), equation~\eqref{eq:inverse-budget} provides the weaker boundary \(\theta+(h-d)/(1+\delta)\). The variables here measure a height as a power of the \emph{starting} scale \(Y\) and an exceptional length on the \emph{logarithmic} axis. An exceptional fraction relative to the whole window would introduce an additional \(\delta\log Y\) factor, which affects finite constants and is absorbed in an arbitrarily small loss in the exponent.

This calculation identifies a specific test for future work. One must exhibit a measurable exceptional set of ordinates, show which arithmetic comparisons hold off it, bound the normalized height on it, and keep the surrounding family fixed. A finite list of excluded weights or primes does not answer the corresponding length question about \(y\). The original construction already supplies a compatible pointwise comparison and never needs this proposed substitute. Our spike functions show that the substitute can be genuinely weaker as a mathematical condition. The real-character measurements give a concrete example of the difference in cost on a particular interval.

\subsection{The contradiction with all choices visible}
Fix a target character and a hypothetical zero of its \(L\)-function in the open strip with real part \(\beta>1/2\). Take a test exponent \(\theta\) strictly between \(1/2\) and \(\beta\). The arithmetic trace supplies the compatible positive family/level containing the target in the cyclic argument and the scale choices that control that level. Its collection of good windows is unbounded. Fixing these data prevents a favorable comparison at one ordinate from being transferred to an unrelated observable at the next.

On the analytic side the zero makes the family's Mellin transform singular. Strict positivity of the family weights prevents the relevant multiplicities from canceling. Localized high derivatives link the singularity to a lower bound on the accumulated positive part in \emph{every} late window, including the ones arithmetic will pick. For any \(0<b<(1+\delta)(\beta-\theta)\), after a sufficiently late threshold that mass exceeds \(Y^b\). On the arithmetic side a chosen good window is pointwise bounded by \(y^\theta\), supplying mass at most \(\delta\log Y\). Select a window beyond both thresholds and beyond the elementary crossover where \(Y^b>\delta\log Y\). Both claims now concern the same real function, weighted in the same way, over exactly the same interval. This is where the putative zero becomes untenable.

For the numeric thought experiment in the opening, \(\beta=3/4,\theta=5/8,\delta=1/2\) give \(b=1/8\) comfortably inside the forced range. Our phase experiment used \(\theta=1/2\) to make the predicted exponent \(3/8\) directly observable in a model. The real-data tables likewise use \(\theta=1/2\) as one of two finite measurement conventions. The arithmetic contradiction chooses a strictly higher test exponent. It can approach \(1/2\) as the putative zero approaches the critical line, by changing the auxiliary tolerances in the allowed order. No uniform endpoint estimate \(G_F(y)\le \sqrt y\) for all large ordinates was required.

\subsection{What this round adds to the series}
The earlier analytic experiments established that the sign of a real residue, the ability to separate nearby poles, and interior measurements all matter. They also reproduced the original conditional analytic implication. This round turns the implication into an explicit positive-area consequence, recovers its sharp model exponent, and brings the same-family arithmetic quantifiers to the point of contradiction. On genuine arithmetic data it resolves a previously missing input, the normalization for a nonprincipal character, and measures the integral statistic over whole finite ranges. The two branches meet at an observable whose mathematical definition, experimental value and necessary quantifiers we can now specify independently.

The next uncertainty lies upstream of this meeting point. Under the actual construction, can an estimate of exceptional \emph{length in the ordinate} be proved before the pointwise comparison is available? If so, the simple inequality above tells us precisely how strong it must be. At present the supported result is the stronger pointwise argument already traced. The experiment has identified both why it works and which evidence a different analytic interface would require.



\appendix
\section{Verification record and reproducibility}
\subsection{Which statements were replayed}
Two new Lean modules address the two ends of the comparison. \texttt{WindowMass.lean} proves integrability for the positive-part density on a compact window, translates an offending zero into an eventual positive-mass obstruction, compares weights on a terminal subwindow, and proves the strict-power conclusion for each \(b<(1+\delta)(\operatorname{Re}\rho-\theta)\). Its four declaration names are \texttt{integrable\_density}, \texttt{forced\_mass}, \texttt{suffix\_weight} and \texttt{forced\_power}. The module is built on the original analytic implication, with 28 imported project modules checked against their recorded source and compiled-object digests. The resulting supplement passed a separate kernel replay.

\texttt{ArithmeticWindow.lean} proves a conditional passage from compatible local witnesses to a common whole-window bound and imports the original pointwise-window theorem for the resulting zero exclusion. It also checks two rescaling and cutoff equivalences. Its four declaration names are \texttt{selectedWindow}, \texttt{zeroFreeFromLocalHosts}, \texttt{fitIff} and \texttt{cutoffIff}. The replay used 30 authenticated compiled project objects in its dependency check. The two object inventories overlap. Adding the counts would misstate how much of the original project was independently checked. An additional source audit verifies 22 arithmetic source files and their line spans at the original pin.

The Lean version was 4.33.1. Both supplemental modules reported the three axioms \texttt{propext}, \texttt{Classical.choice} and \texttt{Quot.sound} for each exported declaration examined here. The arithmetic selection inputs are tracked in source and supplied as hypotheses at the supplemental bridge. This work does not rebuild the entire upstream arithmetic construction or the complete RH development. The computational certificates for the finite real-character windows use outward interval arithmetic implemented in Python. They have a different verification method from the Lean supplements.

\subsection{What is in the source file}
After the end of the typeset manuscript, its single source file contains the vector PDF figures and a base64-encoded ZIP archive of the experiment. LuaLaTeX reads the three figures directly from source memory; no external image, chapter, bibliography or data file is required to compile the PDF. Run LuaLaTeX twice, or run \texttt{latexmk -lualatex -halt-on-error} on the manuscript. The ZIP is for reproducing the numerical tests and optional kernel checks. Compiling the paper runs neither Python nor the experiments.

To extract the archive, put the following short Python code beside the source and execute it, or run the one-line command in the source's opening comments:
\begin{verbatim}
import base64
from pathlib import Path
source = Path("A_Zero_and_the_Prime_Windows.tex").read_bytes()
marker = b"\nBEGIN REPRODUCIBILITY ARCHIVE\n"
payload = source.split(marker, 1)[1].split(
    b"\nEND REPRODUCIBILITY ARCHIVE", 1)[0]
Path("window_evidence.zip").write_bytes(base64.b64decode(payload))
\end{verbatim}
Unzip into an empty directory. From there, \texttt{python3 -B reproduce.py} verifies the packaged file digests, reruns the model, prime and finite arithmetic controls, and compares fresh outputs with the recorded results. The finite checks use the Python standard library. Integer/rational prime certificates are compared exactly; the floating model diagnostics use documented tolerances. The plotted prime curve is an illustration of the same formula sampled at integer ordinates. The certified claims use a continuous treatment of the gaps. The separate figure-making script uses ReportLab if vector figures are to be regenerated from scratch.

\begin{center}\small\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}p{.20\linewidth}Y@{}}
\toprule
Evidence group & Contents and interpretation\\\midrule
\texttt{analytic/}& Four checked mass theorems; pinned dependency manifest; original-source, compiled-object and kernel replay instructions.\\
\texttt{arith/}& Whole-window witness checks; exact rescaling controls; pinned arithmetic line ranges; conditional kernel module and receipt.\\
\texttt{models/}& Pre-run predictions; positive- and negative-residue controls; phase search, signed cancellation, derivative and gamma-tail diagnostics; spike-width and delayed-onset tests.\\
\texttt{primes/}& Prespecified primary runs; actual normalization certificates; continuous integral and peak enclosures; sharper conductor-four integration and support fraction.\\
\bottomrule\end{tabularx}\end{center}
With an original source tree, the pinned Mathlib build, Lean SDK and compiled analytic object tree available, the driver accepts \texttt{--source}, \texttt{--mathlib}, \texttt{--toolchain} and \texttt{--objects}. The archive's guide describes how to identify or rebuild those objects. The computations use the original Git commit \texttt{40e56bab026552e089d7afcfeff08d2defb9c005} and Mathlib \texttt{0df444a360eaa60ab8c11dca51a86af692955474}. These are identifiers, not assumptions of independent mathematical authority. They let a reader see the exact declarations under discussion.

\end{document}
